### [Childhood and Education #20: Phones and Screens](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XA8fMCnwuc45uZYHX/childhood-and-education-20-phones-and-screens)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We have a respite, so I thought I’d tackle various thoughts on children, phones and screens. GPT-5.6-Sol drops tomorrow, and the Fable agents are hard at work.</p>
<p>I’ll start with the other screens, then finish with the phones.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/edtech">EdTech.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/nonedtech">NonEdTech.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/do-not-ban-social-media-outright">Do Not Ban Social Media Outright.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/some-modern-kids-media-is-pretty-great">Some Modern Kids Media Is Pretty Great.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/ban-phones-in-schools-1">Ban Phones In Schools (1).</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/your-offer-is-acceptable">Your Offer Is Acceptable.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/ban-phones-in-schools-2">Ban Phones In Schools (2).</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/screen-time">Screen Time.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/197250666/inappropriate-content">Inappropriate Content.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>EdTech</h4>


<p>Increasingly, when you pick a school, you are picking EdTech. The school will put your child on a tablet or computer, and expect them to learn that way.</p>
<p>In theory, with sufficient assistance and bespoke design and incentive structures, this is The Way. It sure seems way better than ‘sit and listen to a lecture.’</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25395"></span>


</div>
<p>I am especially excited for Alpha School’s version of this, with its bespoke designs and high level of both expectations and continuous human support.</p>
<p>Alas, most people are getting a much worse version, that is much worse than what you could easily improvise at home. I’m less concerned with ‘EdTech provider is bilking the system with its torment nexus’ and more concerned with ‘children assigned to spend their days in the torment nexus.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/moultano/status/2032473900823773533">Ryan Moulton</a>: When you chose a school for your kids, you probably didn’t realize you were choosing educational software, but that choice of software might be more consequential for your kid than the choice of school.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2032547017717854449">Kelsey Piper</a>: A very well-written, very justifiably angry parental reflection on edtech:</p>
<p><a href="https://moultano.wordpress.com/2026/03/12/our-experience-with-i-ready/">Ryan Moulton:</a> When my son was in first grade, he came home from school in tears saying that he hated math. My wife and I are both engineers, so this was the sort of all-hands-on-deck shock that demanded our immediate attention.</p>
<p>Before this my son had loved math. He would demand that we challenge him with math problems to do in his head in the car and over dinner. He loved doing flashcards. He played math games on his tablet unsupervised for hours.</p>
<p>Even now, years later in 4th grade, he has decided he wants to learn calculus, so he insisted I start explaining it to him as best I could in the car, and started working through pre-algebra in Khan Academy on his own. How is it possible that a kid like this had decided he hated math?</p>
<p>His misery was all due to i-Ready, the software product our district had purchased for math work and testing. During that period my kids’ happiness at the end of the school day was entirely determined by how much time their school had made them spend on i-Ready.</p>
<p>If they hadn’t touched i-Ready, they were happy. If they were forced to do it, they were sad. If they had to spend an unusual amount of time on it, they were in tears.</p>
<p>I started asking around to the other kids’ parents, and I heard similar stories from all of them. Their kids described it as torture. Some of them would hide in the bathroom to avoid it. None of the parents felt that their kids were learning anything at all from it.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>I have no disagreement with i-Ready’s goals. The problem is that the software simply doesn’t work.</p>
<p>i-Ready assumes that the student cannot read, that they must be read to very slowly, that they must listen to the same instructions hundreds of times, and that they cannot ever be allowed to have any control over this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2032563133504754171">Kelsey Piper</a>: The article explains why it was particularly bad as employed in this particular school system, but I don’t think iReady is uniquely bad, and a lot of the things that are wrong here are just things about how edtech in general gets used in schools:</p>
<p>Ryan Moulton: “Being bored” in school is now an entirely different experience than it was when I was a kid. Software enables the enforcement of arbitrary rules that no human being would have the heart or foolishness to enforce.</p>
<p>A teacher, faced with a bored student, would not force them to pay rapt attention to an identical lesson 30 times in a row, 5 days a week, for the entirety of the school year. Software can do that easily. A teacher would not demand that all students take an identical amount of time to finish an assignment regardless of how well they’ve mastered the material.</p>
<p>Software can do that easily. A teacher paying attention to a class will adapt to what is working, what is holding their attention, and what is serving their needs. Software is by default thoughtless, and that allows it to be thoughtlessly cruel.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://moultano.wordpress.com/2026/03/12/our-experience-with-i-ready/">The full article is even worse</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dissproportion/status/2032931571422855542">Dissproportionately</a>: My 14yo daughter read this article and then showed me what iReady looked like for her. Even though she’s in the 8th grade it STILL read everything aloud to her at a slow pace, unskippable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlecStapp/status/2033150007549776228">Alec Stapp</a>: This sounds like torture</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/agraybee/status/2033248717084496281">Everything Price Sufferer</a>: The CIA literally experimented with a form of psychological torture that was repeating a recording of the same sentence for 24 hours.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/karenvaites/status/2033144946866188791">Karen Vaites</a>: What if I told you that a @usedgov website was practically marketing iReady Math based on a flawed study conducted during the pandemic?</p>
<p>[goes on to explain more and it only gets worse]</p></blockquote>
<p>The i-Ready system is used by 14 million students, despite being this stupid.</p>
<p>Our system is such that this level of reaction does not result in ‘oh I guess we should either adjust how we use i-Ready or stop making those kids use i-Ready, then.’</p>
<p>Meanwhile Ryan understood the problem, kept raising the problem, and nothing could be done about the problem.</p>
<p>Ryan literally had to move in order to get his child away from this program, but couldn’t do this until after an entire school year.</p>
<p>How are these schools not being burned to the ground? Or at least having all their students flee in horror?</p>
<p>If this is something that can survive indefinitely, how else do we torture our kids?</p>
<p>Would a teacher force a student to sit in class, 5 days a week, for the whole school year, while you gave a lesson the student already knew and got no value from? Surely the teacher would adjust for that, right?</p>
<p>I mean, maybe, but in many cases no, absolutely not. I speak from personal experience. You can absolutely be assigned such a class in school, as I was, and spend an entire year learning almost nothing, but at least it is not literally the identical lesson 30 times in a row 5 days a week and it wasn’t literal torture.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.slowboring.com/p/ed-tech-is-not-the-answer-or-the">Matthew Yglesias tries to defend i-Ready</a>, saying his son’s school uses it better so it is not that bad, but to me that only says that school is otherwise so torturous that ‘forced to listen to the same explanations in slow motion over and over again all the time while doing almost zero math’ did not rise to the level of a major complaint. That’s worse, you know why that’s worse, right?</p>
<p>I do agree with the broader point he’s trying to make, that edTech can be good if used well and you should look at the incentives of the system. But, well, look at how it is being used and at the incentives of the system. What a system.</p>


<h4>NonEdTech</h4>


<p>Even if you do your job at home policing screens, you probably send your kid to school.</p>
<p>Bad EdTech is one problem. Outright hours of random YouTube is another.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/marcportermagee/status/2031316361280946402">Marc Porter Magee</a>: I don’t think a lot of people understand how bad it has gotten in elementary school. Parents are fighting to keep their kids away from screens and junk videos only to have their public schools give away the game</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AConcernedPare2/status/2031369981682700719">AConcernedParent</a>: We banned YouTube at home only to find out my son was spending 5 hours a day watching YouTube shorts at school on his laptop.</p></blockquote>
<p>Worst of all, <a href="https://t.co/TnKlSbJNZc">they clearly aren’t even paying for premium</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>​NYTimes: A few months before her daughter started kindergarten, Claire Benoist saw a Facebook post that stunned her. Another family with an incoming kindergartner was wondering if it was true that children in the Croton-Harmon School District, 40 miles north of New York City, receive iPads when they start school.</p>
<p>Other parents confirmed that during school, kindergartners often used iPads to play games and watch television shows and YouTube videos. School administrators assured Ms. Benoist that iPad time would be limited to 15 minutes a day, she said. But once school started, her daughter suddenly knew jingles from the diaper and car commercials that would play before YouTube videos she saw in the classroom.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Do Not Ban Social Media Outright</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/KevinTFrazier/status/2021911834542940662">What I oppose as a solution to all this is bans on social media before a fixed age</a>, which is usually 16. Social media bans require age verification techniques that are easy to evade, require large invasions of privacy and lead to frequent leaking of data, and this is a violation of free speech and the ability of children to communicate and in some cases has ended up even extending to Substack. The places kids go instead will often be worse rather than better. This decision should at maximum be up to parents.</p>
<p>Australia tried a ban. <a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/the-social-media-ban-in-australia-how-is-it-going.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=the-social-media-ban-in-australia-how-is-it-going">It isn’t going great</a>, because you need to hit critical mass, and they failed. Thus, everyone thinks that everyone else is circumventing the ban, therefore everyone circumvents the ban, what like it’s hard?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/the-social-media-ban-in-australia-how-is-it-going.html">Tyler Cowen</a>: A few days ago I was talking with a very smart fifteen year old in Australia (really). He was of the opinion that it was quite ineffective, though he noted he could no longer access LinkedIn.</p>
<p>I would note there are more stringent measures, requiring more governmental monitoring and control of the internet, that perhaps could have a greater effect.</p></blockquote>
<p>The ban including sites like LinkedIn makes it much less likely to stick, because there is far more reason to circumvent the ban. Even if you are happy to give up Instagram and TikTok, especially provided other kids also do so, are you willing to sacrifice half of the internet?</p>
<p>There is one other common argument against such bans that I think is quite bad, which is the ‘why is 16 years old different from 15 years and 364 days’ question. I could ask why one extra day sometimes lets you drink, or vote, or consent to sex. You have to draw a line somewhere, and we presumably can agree that the minimum age for social media or those other activities should not be zero.</p>
<p>I also don’t think rhetoric like this does advocates any favors:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/mbateman/status/2021723678078378212">Matt Bateman</a>: “Should children be banned from any exposure to the ultra giga turbo Republic of Letters in which the human spirit is enmeshed and by means of which it is evolving” should obviously be answered in the negative.</p></blockquote>
<p>Nor do I think that correlational studies should convince us to defy our lying eyes.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/reason/status/2021570443111465284">reason</a>: For boys, staying off social media might be worse than heavy use, according to a new study. For both girls and boys, using social media *moderately* was tied to better well-being than either abstinence or heavy use.</p></blockquote>
<p>In the full article, Elizabeth Brown is unusually excellent here about explaining why, and I am glad to report the post is generally very good epistemically.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://reason.com/2026/02/09/a-goldilocks-effect-for-online-teens-moderate-social-media-users-fare-better-than-abstainers-or-heavy-users/">Elizabeth Brown</a> (Reason): The data revealed “a U-shaped association,” where both social media abstinence and heavy social media use were linked to poorer well-being while moderate social media use was linked to better well-being.</p>
<p>“Among girls, well-being was highest with no use in early adolescence (grades 4–6), but from middle adolescence (grades 7–9) onward moderate use was most advantageous, while high use was consistently adverse and had the greatest association with low well-being in grades 8 and 9,” it elaborates.</p>
<p>“Among boys, well-being was similar for nonusers and moderate users in early adolescence (grades 4–6), but from mid-adolescence (grades 7–9) nonuse became increasingly associated with poorer outcomes, exceeding the risk of high use by late adolescence (grades 10–12).”</p>
<p>… It’s possible that well-adjusted young people with healthy home lives and happy social lives are prone to neither spend too much time on social media nor to avoid it entirely.</p>
<p>… In other words, poorer or better well-being may drive the amount of social media use rather than the other way around—or some third factor (such as super-strict parents) may drive both the amount of time spent online and overall levels of well being.</p></blockquote>
<p>Brown also points to the years the data covered: 2020 to 2022, which were perhaps the worst time in history to try and go offline.</p>
<p>Here’s a rather scary paragraph:</p>
<blockquote><p>Keep in mind that the “moderate use” category in this study was not conservative. Up to 12.5 hours per week of social media use between the weekday hours of 3 and 6 p.m. was defined as moderate. So this isn’t merely a finding that the smallest smidge of social media is OK.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are only 15 weekday hours between 3pm and 6pm. So the students who are ‘heavy’ users need an intervention, and we should freak out that 12.5 hours out of 15 should be considered ‘moderate’ use.</p>
<p>This ‘moderate’ use also includes students who use social media any nonzero amount.</p>


<h4>Some Modern Kids Media Is Pretty Great</h4>


<p>You can also offer YouTube via whitelisting channels. It can be done.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jasoncrawford/status/2023838426252943705">Jason Crawford</a>: I can empathize with this, and I feel sorry for anyone struggling with digital media and kids. But—</p>
<p>In our household, digital media is a net positive. There are many healthy YouTube channels for our 4yo to watch. Numberblocks is great on math concepts, Daniel Tiger has many helpful life lessons, and even Curious George is wholesome fun. For sure, you have to whitelist the channels, and not let the algorithm descend into slop.</p>
<p>Audiobooks are also great, and access to all kinds of music (mostly Disney), and games. Again, whitelisted, with parental controls.</p>
<p>Maybe this changes for older kids. And probably we have an unusually disciplined toddler who does not get unduly addicted to the iPad. And of course we haven’t gone anywhere near social media yet.</p>
<p>But overall, so far, I’m quite happy to be parenting in the age of digital media.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/oscredwin/status/2024094238560989635">Andrew Rettek</a>: Numberblocks is the best kids show I’ve ever seen!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TaraAnnThieke/status/2023842551808278616">Tara Ann Thieke</a>: Numberblocks is amazing. There was a review a few months back which rated Bluey, Paw Patrol etc and Numberblocks was the highest achiever by far. My 4 year old started doing addition all day after a few episodes.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are some pretty great kids shows now. The production quality these days is off the charts, and some of them really are excellent with the content, especially Numberblocks and Bluey. You also can dip into much of the archives of your own childhood, and a lot of it will not age well. The problem is avoiding the kids getting exposed or attached to the stuff that isn’t good and then demanding it or sneaking it.</p>
<p>You also can pick from the best of the past, on the cheap, and on demand.</p>


<h4>Ban Phones In Schools (1)</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w35132#fromrss">Allcott et al looked in an unpublished study at impacts </a>from lockable pouches for phones, which I would have thought would be the right way to implement a phone ban. They don’t find much impact in the first year.</p>
<blockquote><p>In the first year after adoption, disciplinary incidents increase and student subjective well-being falls, consistent with short-term disruption. However, effects on well-being become positive in later years and disciplinary effects fade.</p>
<p>For academic achievement, average effects on test scores are consistently close to zero. High schools see modest positive effects, particularly in math, while middle schools see small negative effects.</p>
<p>We find little evidence of effects on school attendance, self-reported classroom attention, or perceived online bullying.​</p></blockquote>
<p>In other studies I recall effects tending to improve in subsequent years, as everyone adapts to the new equilibrium, and we see that here as well. Well-being turns positive over time, as does discipline, and it stands to reason this will cash out in academics.</p>
<p>To the extent that this does not reduce student well-being, but also does not substantially improve student academics, that is basically saying that attention paid to school does not improve academics, and that time with phones does not improve well-being. So again it sounds like we should ban, for children, both phones and schools.</p>
<p><a href="https://grimoiremanor.substack.com/p/a-new-study-spells-bad-news-for-cellphone?r=un33&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web&amp;triedRedirect=true">Christopher Ferguson analyzes the results in detail</a>, noticing that the authors are being naive frequentists about their conclusions and he questions their statistical approach in other ways as well. He thinks they’re trying to put the thumb on the scale to favor bans and in spite of this still came up with nothing, then coauthor Thomas Dee went to the press saying that this nothing supported bans, warning that some people might draw the opposite conclusion.</p>
<p>The pouches at least are shown to reduce actual use, whereas for most attempted bans the real answer comes from this, another study by Henry Saffer:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w35181#fromrss">Henry Saffer</a>: The outcome variables are screentime and measures of psychological wellbeing. Overall, these early results provide no clear evidence that the school ban policy reduced screentime or improved psychological wellbeing.</p></blockquote>
<p>Read that again. Banning cell phones in schools had no impact on screen time? What?</p>
<p>I realize that time shifting can be a thing. Kids could in theory be getting more screen time outside of school to compensate for it in school. But the more obvious conclusion is that the bans simply aren’t being enforced at all. So of course they don’t work. Which is indeed a reason not to bother with current bans in their current form.</p>
<p>Here is another perspective, and given the need to keep good teachers it matters:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://arnoldkling.substack.com/p/culture-links-5102026">Arnold Kling</a>: Students using phones in school makes teachers feel disrespected. I am quite certain of that, even if the evidence about the educational benefit of phone bans is contested. If teachers’ feelings count, then as long as getting rid of phones at school doesn’t make things worse for students you can increase aggregate utility by banning them.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Your Offer Is Acceptable</h4>


<blockquote><p>Eli Stark-Elster: Major public intellectuals and politicians have responded by arguing that children should rarely, if ever, participate in digital spaces. As a result, many schools in the US now demand that students seal their smartphones in magnetic pouches. A number of countries, including Australia, the United Kingdom and France, are even considering or have already implemented bans on social media accounts for children and teenagers.</p>
<p>Such restrictions, however, are not the tools of liberation we may imagine them to be. In fact, for some children, the internet may be one of the last remaining spaces where they can grow up doing what children everywhere have evolved to do: independently play and explore with their peers.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/have-online-worlds-become-the-last-free-places-for-children.html">Tyler Cowen</a>: Here is <a href="https://psyche.co/ideas/have-online-worlds-become-the-last-free-places-for-children">more from anthropologist Eli Stark-Elster</a>. I would add a point. I do accept the evidence suggesting that limiting or banning cell phones in schools brings marginally better academic results.</p>
<p>Yet the people who advocate such policies never point out that so many schools are just deadly dull and not very intellectually stimulating? Often what is on the phone is in fact more interesting and sometimes more instructive as well, even if the students do worse in terms of the standards set by the school.</p></blockquote>
<p>Great point. If phone bans in school don’t do much then school doesn’t do much.</p>
<p>Similarly, <a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w35233#fromrss">a study from Brazil measures impact of a 2023 policy</a> banning non-pedagogical uses of phones in schools in Rio, and shows it lowered phone use substantially but only improved test scores by 0.06 standard deviations.</p>
<p>So, Tyler, your offer is acceptable. We should ban phones from school, and also ban school, by which I mean ban mandatory school. If you choose to go, that’s fine too.</p>
<p>Instead, in response to the study in the previous section, Tyler Cowen seems to think this means we should let kids be on their phones all day instead of school, don’t worry about it, but do still force the kids to be in school while they are doing this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/05/the-best-study-to-date-on-school-phone-bans.html">Tyler Cowen</a>: In sum, it is fine to want to run a school that way, but do not expect huge educational gains, if any. The evidence on this is accumulating, but many seem unable to accept the results. In any case it is not worthy of a major moral crusade.</p></blockquote>
<p>Saying that a child has to physically be present in a particular location, on point of men with guns showing up to their house, but then when at that location they can be on their phone watching random AI generated short form video scrolls?</p>
<p>That seems beyond vile. Loser mindset makes no sense to me.</p>
<p>The counterargument is that it does seem like school is better than being under a Covid lockdown, in terms of educational outcomes. Something happens somewhere, even if it is woefully inefficient at doing it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/LexerLux/status/2051349829884776664">Lexer</a>: Everyone is struggling to understand how evidence shows school phone bans don’t improve outcomes when phones are clearly ruining schools.<br />
Except me, because I never believed school improved outcomes to begin with.<br />
Are you ready to take the caplanpill, Anon?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2051350553482117622">Kelsey Piper</a>: We ran a big experiment where we shut down the schools and it turned out this resulted in a massive decrease in student performance. question settled imo.</p>
<p>I think schools teach literacy, numeracy, and a bunch of broad background on the world which is mostly invisible to us because we don’t remember not knowing it (like that the country has a President, had a civil war, the Moon doesn’t glow but reflects light, etc).</p>
<p>Intellectually curious people will pick up a ton of that last thing regardless and school often doesn’t succeed at being an efficient way to teach it, but school does teach it and it does matter.</p>
<p>Now, can you design a program that teaches 4x as much of it in the same time? Yes! I believe what we’re doing is at least that much better! But when you meet people who do not have it, it does seem to me to be holding them back in life.</p></blockquote>
<p>To which the response is, well, even if you are okay with normal school, zoom school is actively destructive and also permanently traumatizing against authority and education and created a situation of permanent widespread truancy. But hey.</p>


<h4>Ban Phones In Schools (2)</h4>


<p>The anecdotal evidence seems to indicate bans work for common sense reasons, even if measured outcomes did not improve in the short term.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2001464352491311196">Kevin Roose</a>: I confess I was not totally convinced that the phone bans would work, but early evidence suggests a total @JonHaidt victory.</p></blockquote>
<p>Kevin links to a <a href="https://archive.is/AJIp3">New York Magazine</a> article by Anya Kamenetz, with teachers and parents noticing kids responding to the phone ban in New York by acting like kids again. They’re playing board and card games and sports, they’re getting to know each other and hanging out, ‘willing to do more stuff.’</p>
<p>I love my old Stuyvesant High School, where the objection is you need your phone to study and do homework, never change:</p>
<blockquote><p>Anya Kamenetz: Noshin Sayira is a junior at Stuyvesant High School, meaning she’s in the middle of the highest-pressure year at what may be one of the highest-pressure high schools in the country. She tells me that students’ top objection to the phone policy is that it’s become cumbersome to do homework between classes or to quickly study in the hallway before a test.</p>
<p>But Noshin recently started printing out her study guides and has found that reviewing on paper actually works better: “I don’t get distracted by notifications.”</p></blockquote>


<h4>Screen Time</h4>


<p>By default, screen time will go to whatever wins the attention war. It’s a minefield out there. Given free rein most young children will inevitably end up choosing poorly.</p>
<p>If a service includes the option to navigate into slop, that means you can’t allow it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2023614697061110041">PoIiMath</a>: I’m so frustrated at being a parent in this garbage digital age.</p>
<p>I hate short-form video. TikTok / YouTube trash is just pure brain-rot and I can’t stand it. I try to keep my kids away from it.</p>
<p>I gave them access to Spotify b/c I want them to enjoy music and develop their musical tastes and personalities. That was working pretty well. They would go onto Spotify on the XBox and listen to music and explore that space. Good for them.</p>
<p>But what does Spotify do? They put short form videos into their app. Now my kids are watching the videos instead of listening to the music. I have to decide if I have to take Spotify away from them (along with all their playlists) because Spotify pulled this bait-and-switch on me and turned an app that I felt good about giving to my kids into another brain-rotting platform of garbage.</p>
<p>Every month something like this happens. It’s impossible to navigate this as a parent, even if you’re largely on top of things. It’s exhausting and dispiriting.</p></blockquote>
<p>For that specific situation, there are three reasonable known solutions.</p>
<ol>
<li>Give Spotify access from a Google Home or other device that lacks a screen.</li>
<li>Spotify does let you turn the video option off via Settings and Privacy → Content and Display → “Videos and Canvas.” In theory they can change the setting back, but seems reasonable to tell them not to. You can’t turn off video ads this way, so for the full effect you’ll have to pay up.</li>
<li>You can transfer the playlists using cheap tools or vibecode your own, and go to another platform that lacks the issue. The problem is that all of the major competitors have a version of the same issue, so you’d have to go to something that requires more work.</li>
</ol>
<p>That ‘more work’ option is still way ahead of what we had growing up.</p>
<p>The problem is that we have really good options that come with really bad options that act as attractor states that you have to fend off. You still have the old options in various forms, you can buy a VCR and some tapes or an old iPod and download songs, but for good reasons you don’t want to.</p>


<h4>Inappropriate Content</h4>


<p>Moving beyond any given app or service to the general case: Parents want to be able to offer their children the use of computers and phones and televisions and so on, and send their kids to school, while having some control over what types of media is viewed and applications are used.</p>
<p>The world does not make this easy.</p>
<p>Instead, we are getting a bunch of age verification laws, which leak private information and are trivial to bypass, and also don’t actually address most of the important concerns, which are things like short form video and other slop. That’s way worse than ‘social media’ which basically means we aren’t letting kids communicate with each other while we also don’t let them travel to meet up in person.</p>
<p>What do you expect is going to happen?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TimSweeneyEpic/status/2026294470140928307">Tim Sweeney</a>: Apple is the only company with a great proposal here: let parents, who buy the devices used by kids, set up kids accounts and decide what they’re allowed to do, then pass those decisions to apps through parental controls — without demanding anyone’s identity papers.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2026702129625534958">PoIiMath</a>: When it comes to raising children, every tech dork and libertarian is like “omg, just parent your children” and every parent is like “Why does Apple’s screen time feature not work as advertised?”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2026703156529275096">PoIiMath</a>: “all you have to do is parent your children!</p>
<p>“All you have to do is follow this 12 step process to manage access permissions on your iPad”</p>
<p>“All you have to do is block these CDNs with this DNS service”</p>
<p>“All you have to do is disconnect every device from the Internet”</p>
<p>“All you have to do is move to the woods and live as a monk”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mrsnightskyre/status/2026705435726586121">Anitra (Yes, with an “R”)</a>: “All you have to do is trust that the school’s VPN will definitely block inappropriate content to the school-issued iPad that you have no control over.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ckayerawlings/status/2029262636903481692">gal debored</a>: I read that teens would be willing to get off their phones more if their parents let them go anywhere alone. They’d even settle for hanging out in their own front yards unsupervised. But parents overwhelmingly won’t allow it. I don’t think therapy can do much in the face of that.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/naranciagaming/status/2029450096266408189">narancia gaming</a>: it is truly incredible how abusively controlling 90% of modern parents are<br />
being on their children’s and their TEENAGERS’ asses 24/7, tracking their every move with surveillance apps – that is, if they’re even allowed to leave the house and meet with friends at all</p>
<p>and now they’re coming for their online presence – the only place where they still have any semblance of freedom. frankly i don’t think the online space is good for kids, but once they’re kicked off of social media, what the hell will they even be able to do?</p>
<p>can’t meet up with friends to do stuff in person, can’t talk to friends on twitter or discord… it’s like they’re actively trying to create the man with nothing to lose :(</p></blockquote>


<h4>Now Here’s The Deal</h4>


<p>The solution to all of this is obvious.</p>
<ol>
<li>Get phones out of schools, whether or not kids have to stay in them.</li>
<li>Give parents real device-level controls over what apps and settings can be used.</li>
<li>Give kids back their physical freedom of action and ability to be kids in real life.</li>
</ol>
<p> </p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XA8fMCnwuc45uZYHX/childhood-and-education-20-phones-and-screens#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Fragments: July  6](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-07-06.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Last week, Thoughtworks ran a second <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">Future of Software Development Retreat</a>, this time in Europe. As with the previous event, I’ll be sharing some fragmentary thoughts on this. There were five parallel streams, so I could, at best, only attend ⅕ of sessions. This isn’t an event that forms conclusions, rather one that allows those exploring to share what they’ve found, and their visions for the future. The <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">bliki post</a> lists all the writing I’ve run into on this, by myself and others. I’ll be updating it as more posts appear.</p>

<p>Giles Edwards-Alexander <a href="https://overwatering.org/blog/2026/07/notes-from-fose-europe/">“noticed a real difference between the retreats”</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Where Deer Valley had hesitancy and a belief that there was something here even if we weren’t yet sure what it was, Engelberg had confidence: the value is here. As I explained to a colleague today, this was not a conference for true believers: the evidence is in.</p>

  <p>What does the evidence say? Well, that was less clear. Some patterns and practices are emerging (one attendee had catalogued dozens of agentic engineering pattern libraries) but they are emerging. There is more work to do to truly establish what is effective, and when.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Greg Herlein <a href="https://blog.herlein.com/post/retreat-observations/">felt similarly</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Reading the reports of the February event, when a lot of these same folks last got together, the conversation was about what agentic development might look like. Aspirational. More about what was coming.</p>

  <p>This time? Everybody in the room was doing it. Shipping it. Not slides - production. The whole debate about whether this changes software engineering is over. People have stopped arguing about whether a while ago. They’re arguing about how, and the how is getting real.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>On a more micro level, I noted two other things. Firstly, there was much talk now about <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">harness engineering</a>, when that wasn’t even a term in Utah - an example of how rapidly things are moving. Secondly people are now worrying about the cost of tokens, where before folks were wanting to do almost anything to incentivize people to talk to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/who-is-llm.html">The Genie</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>A question that continued from Utah was whether architecture and design are still important. There seems to be two landmark hypotheses here, one is that The Genie has such a Galaxy Brain that we no longer need to care about such matters, it will handle as much spaghetti as we can throw at it. The other is, in Laura Tacho’s <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-02-13.html">memorable phrase</a>: “the Venn Diagram of Developer Experience and Agent Experience is a circle”. The point being that The Genie uses the same constructs to understand a code base that humans do, so things like good modularity and naming help it as much as it helps humans. Adam Tornhill’s writing is a <a href="https://adamtornhill.substack.com/p/welcome-to-code-for-humans-and-machines">good example</a> of this viewpoint.</p>

<p>Tidbits from our session on this:</p>

<ul>
  <li>to evaluate the value of architecture we need to focus on desirable outcomes. Internal design quality boils down to <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/is-quality-worth-cost.html">ease of change</a>. The question is whether the lessons we’ve learned so far will continue for agents.</li>
  <li>a way to measure design quality is to look at token costs. If the same change requires less tokens that indicates a better architecture.</li>
  <li>a good architecture only shows its quality over time, we can’t easily measure it in the short term</li>
  <li>why did 3GL languages continue when things like 4GLs, UML etc not take hold? It’s because these programming languages hit a sweet spot of human comprehension of computation</li>
  <li>we’re at the first time ever where the computers care about code quality</li>
  <li>will future models write machine code directly? If so what will humans review or specify?</li>
  <li>we should beware of speculating about what LLMs may do in the future. Instead we need <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/MechanicalSympathy.html">mechanical sympathy</a> for our LLMs, so we can gain a sense of how they work and how best to use them.</li>
  <li>One workflow:
    <ul>
      <li>take story from backlog</li>
      <li>talk it over with an agent</li>
      <li>once get an agreement, make an ADR for persistent record of spec</li>
      <li>generate a task list</li>
      <li>get agent to complete it</li>
    </ul>
  </li>
  <li>we need abstractions to communicate with agents <em>(echoing Unmesh Joshi’s thoughts on <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html">building conceptual models</a>)</em></li>
  <li>we often find duplication in LLM generated code, together with mixing of concerns (eg intermingled domain and display logic) - even with a good harness</li>
  <li>get agents to generate explanatory documentation at the end of a session</li>
  <li>overnight quality checks with a report for humans to act on in the morning</li>
  <li>LLMs look at existing code, so if that code has problems, the LLM will amplify them</li>
  <li>we should be wary of drawing too many conclusions comparing LLM code with human code - human code varies enormously from team to team.</li>
</ul>

<p> ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In his account of the retreat, Mathias Verraes goes into the details of <a href="https://verraes.net/2026/07/software-design-in-the-agentic-age/">his perspective of these issues of software design</a>. He adds another concern: we need good design as a hedge against the risk of dependence on AI. After all, we don’t know how high the costs may rise to. We see governments blocking access to models. We see popular opposition to AI campaigning against data centers and calling for regulation. How much can we rely on AI tools being available to maintain and extend our software in the future?</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Charity Majors has a post on <a href="https://charitydotwtf.substack.com/p/make-ai-boring-again">the ethics of working with AI</a> and does an excellent job of articulating how I feel about this topic. She outlines the harms inherent in AI, both in the creation of its models (training on stolen data) and in inference (slop, lack of accountability, skill atrophy). Her conclusion however, like mine, is that there’s no ethical gain from renouncing the use of AI and castigating those who use it. Such purity provides little practical help with a technology that is so powerful and so useful.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The way you show care is by showing up. The way you make the world a better place is by getting down in the muck and building it, using whatever skills and resources you have on hand. The way you drive change is you engage.</p>

  <p>Yes, we are all complicit. Yes, we are all compromised. No argument. But what are you going to do with that feeling of conviction? Will you channel your discomfort into solidarity and action, or try to ease your conscience by removing yourself from the system? Which does more to help those being harmed?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Her suggestions on how to engage aren’t striking, but that’s hardly unusual. At the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">Future of Software Development Retreat</a> I convened a session on this question, and nothing striking turned up there either. That said, I’ve never been much of an activist, so my imagination may be limited.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Gergely Orosz has run into a case where an article of his was <a href="https://blog.pragmaticengineer.com/pollen-tried-to-remove-my-article-about-callum-negus-fancey-and-google-is-assisting-to-it/">erased from Google search</a> by a clearly fraudulent DMCA claim.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It seems that anyone can file a bogus copyright claim to get an article they don’t like removed from Google’s search index. This happened in this case. I have no information on who filed the copyright claim. Even less so on who claims to be the copyright owner? Because I am the only possible copyright owner!</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He was able to find the DMCA complaint, it was made by “Ellie Piee” whose profile listed them as living on Bouvet Island, an uninhabited Norwegian dependent territory near Antarctica. It claimed Gergely’s article copied a New York Post article entitled “Band Leader Hits Winning Chord”. But Gergely’s article is “Inside Pollen’s Collapse: “$200M Raised” but Staff Unpaid”, and the two do not share a single sentence. There’s an obvious motivation for folks connected with Pollen to have done this, and I hope the resulting <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streisand_effect?ref=blog.pragmaticengineer.com">Streisand effect</a> bites them where it hurts.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>404 media have a bunch of (paywalled) reports on the impact of companies realizing that token costs are getting out of control. They’ve acquired <a href="https://www.404media.co/companies-are-throttling-employees-ai-use-because-its-too-expensive/">leaked Slack chats, internal dashboards, emails and other material</a> from companies including Citi and Amazon.</p>

<p>Companies are urging staff to use less powerful models, or cutting off frontier models entirely. A dashboard indicates that one company has seen its token bill rise from $5 million in August 2025 to $15 million in May 2026, on track to spend over $120 million in the fiscal year.</p>

<p>404 earlier reported about <a href="https://www.404media.co/the-tokenpocalypse-is-here-companies-are-scrambling-to-stop-spending-so-much-on-ai/">Accenture taking steps to reduce token usage</a>. The biggest problem wasn’t software engineering using <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a>, but rather staff “chewing tokens” by using AI to do things like turning PDFs into presentation slides. They saw themselves, and their clients, grappling with exponential increases in token costs. Inevitably, after consulting firms spent time urging their clients to use AI heavily, they are now offering services to control these costs.</p>

<p>Another post says it appears that one way to reduce token costs is to get AI tools to <a href="https://www.404media.co/companies-are-making-claude-and-codex-talk-like-cavemen-to-stop-ais-soaring-costs/">speak like cavemen</a>, using a <a href="https://github.com/JuliusBrussee/caveman?ref=404media.co">skill/plugin</a>.</p>

<p>There’s a good summary of all this on 404’s freely available podcast: <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Sia4LZGNkVs">The AI Tokenpocalypse Is Here</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I share these thoughts just after the July 4th weekend here in America, indeed the Semiquincentennial. Historian Bret Devereaux celebrated this event with <a href="https://acoup.blog/2026/07/04/collections-on-the-declaration-of-independence/">a careful reading of the Declaration of Independence</a>, a document often talked about more than it’s read. Which is a shame since it is hardly very long, and its impact was remarkable, and not just in what is now the United States.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The Declaration of Independence was recognized as a radical, potentially explosive document at the time of its issuance, as we’ll see. And it was explosive: the world of 1775 was one dominated by monarchies with just a tiny handful of traditional republics (which we should not ignore!). It took a long time for the seeds of the declaration to spread, but the world it helped create is one where liberal democracies, while hardly universal (more people have always lived in unfree societies than free ones) represent the most economically and culturally dominant bloc in world affairs – something that had never happened before. The Declaration, in its way, remade not just the Thirteen Colonies, but slowly, surely, as water seeps through the cracks of rocks (or my floorboards, alas), it remade the whole world.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Devereaux shines a light onto the world of this text, illuminating its historic context, a world that is very different to the one anyone reading this grew up in. It’s assertions of a natural law that there is equality of rights among men and that governments ought to derive their powers from the consent of the governed would seem hardly worth stating now, yet were deeply radical in 1776. I’ve found that reading history like this has helped me understand how the world is, and gives me a broader perspective on the drama of current affairs.</p>

---
